Die 16a Septembris 1941. 116

INTRODUCTION 

For the first time we are able to present a complete, dependable, and up-to-date survey of all the orders and congregations of St. Francis of Assisi in the United States. The National Secretary of the Third Order issued a directory of the Third Order Secular in 1931, and another in 1935; this time he adds information also concerning the various families and communities of the First Order, the Second Order, and the Third Order Regular. 

Attempts have been made to list the various Franciscan religious communities along with others in the annual editions of the Official 

Catholic Directory, the Official Catholic Year Book of 1928, and Mrs. 

Tehey's Relgious Orders of Women in the United States (1930); and though these attempts deserve to be commended, they have not proved altogether satisfactory. Additional information has been gathered and published in Franciscan Herald of 1929 and 1930 in a series of articles on Franciscan Sisterhoods under the general caption "Children of the Poverello"; in the 1936 Report of the Franciscan Educational Conference, containing a statistical study of the provinces of the First Order and a survey of the regular Third Order; and during recent years in Third

Order Forum and Franciscan Herald and Forum. While these investigations have all helped to prepare the way, the present survey has been compiled from information officially supplied by the headquarters of the various Franciscan communities and Third Order fraternites. 

The information contained in these pages offers abundant proof that the Franciscan ideal is a fruitful ideal in modern American life To some it may appear strange that there are so many different and autonomous religious communities, all of whom honor St. Francis as their spiritual father and observe one of his rules. But this very fact argues for the versatility of the Franciscan vocation. Far from hindering, it has promoted the development and expansion of the Franciscan movement in the United States. Had there been but one organization, it would never have attained such a widespread development in such a short time. There is no need, therefore, that various groups of a similar nature be merged. 

However, the time has come for the different branches of the Franciscan Order to become better acquainted with one another. They all have one thing in common, and that is the Franciscan ideal; why should they not unify their forces, in some way at least, to foster and promote that ideal? Those who live in religious communities have, moreover, one task in common: and that is the propagation of the Third Order 

PAGE 5

PAGE 6 THE FIRST ORDER 

Secular and, through the latter, of the spirit of St. Francis among the faithful living outside the convent.

Since the establishment of the National Organization of the Third Order Secular twenty years ago, it was the Third Order above all that has brought together the three branches of the First Order and the Third Order Regular, strictly so-called, in unified action and co-operation. The Second Order has always contributed its share to the spread of the Franciscan movement by its prayers. And now the various sisterhoods and brotherhoods of the regular Third Order have also begun to participate in the work of carrying the spirit of St. Francis into their numerous and diversified secular contacts. 

Who if not the children of St. Francis should heed the call of Pope

Leo XIII and his successors, who have so repeatedly and insistently recommended the Third Order Secular as a sorely needed instrument of social reconstruction? What a powerful impulse will be given to secular Tertiarism in this country, if all the regular brothers and sisters of St. Francis are determined to establish, guide, and in every way promote the Third Order of St. Francis among the charges in their numerous institutions?

Enlightened Christian wisdom will suggest to convent communities that youth disciplined in the world through a rule of life is ready-made material for the religious life in the cloister. 

This survey is then a token of the encouraging progress that the Franciscan leaven has made in the United States; and at the same time. it points the way for further progress in the future. Perhaps more than in the day of St. Francis, the world of today is in dire need of the 

Franciscan ideal.

Quincy College 

Quincy, Illinois.

FR. MARION HABIG, O.F.M.

PART ONE THE FIRST AND SECOND ORDER

The Rule of the Friars Minor BULL OF POPE HONORIUS III 

November 29, 1223

Honorius Bishop, servant of the servants of God. To the beloved sons Brother Francis, and to the other brethren of the Order of Friars 

Minor, health and apostolic benediction. The Apostolic See is wont to assent to pious wishes, and graciously to comply with the reasonable desires of supplicants. Wherefore, beloved sons in the Lord, yielding to your pious entreaties, we confirm unto you by apostolic authority and strengthen with the patronage of this present writing, the Rule of your Order herein contained, approved by our predecessor of happy memory Pope Innocent, which is as follows:

CHAPTER I. 

[In the Name of the Lord Begins the Life of the Friars Minor]

The rule and life of the Friars Minor is this, namely, to observe the holy Gospel of Our Lord Jesus Christ, by living in obedience, without property and in chastity. Brother Francis promises obedience and reverence to the Lord Pope Honorius and to his successors canonically elected, and to the Roman Church, and let the other brethren be bound to obey Brother Francis and his successors.

CHAPTER II. 

[Of Those Who Wish to Embrace This Life and How They Should Be Received] 

If any wish to embrace this life and come to our brethren, let them be directed to the Provincial Ministers, to whom alone, and to no others, may authority be given for receiving brethren. Let the Ministers examine them carefully in the Catholic Faith and in the sacraments of the Church. If their faith therein is found to be complete and they purpose loyally to profess it and firmly to keep it to the end; and, if they are unmarried, or should they be married-if their wives have already entered a convent, or, at least, with their Bishop's sanction, have taken a vow of chastity and given their consent, and are, moreover, of such an age that no suspicion can attach to them, then let them (the Ministers) declare to them the words of the holy Gospel that they go and sell all they have, purposing to give to the poor; and if they cannot do this, their good will suffices.

PAGE 9

PAGE 

Let the brethren and their Ministers be careful not to interfere in their temporal concerns, so that they may be free to dispose of their goods in whatever way the Lord shall inspire them. If, however, advice is asked, let the Ministers be empowered to send them to some Godfearing persons by whose advice their goods may be distributed to the poor. Afterwards let them give them the habit of probation, viz., two tunics, without a hood, a cord and breeches and a caparon reaching to the cord, unless at any time the same Ministers should decide otherwise, according to God. When the year of probation is ended, let them be received to obedience, promising to observe always this form of life and Rule; and in no way shall it be lawful for them to leave this Order, according to the command of the Lord Pope, for, as the Gospel says: 

No man, putting his hand to the plough and looking back is fit for the Kingdom of God.

Those who have promised obedience may have one tunic with a hood, and, such as wish to have one, another without a hood. Those who are compelled by necessity may wear shoes. Let all the brethren be clad in poor garments, and they may patch them with sackcloth and other pieces, with the blessing of God. I warn and exhort them not to despise nor judge those whom they see clad in apparel soft and colored, and using choice food and drink; but rather let each one judge and despise himself.

CHAPTER III. 

[Of the Divine Office and Fasting, and of How the Brethren Should Behave in the World] 

Let the clerics recite the Divine Office according to the rite of the Holy Roman Church, except for the Psalter; and for this they may have breviaries. Let the lay-brothers say twenty-four Pater Noster for 

Matins, five for Lauds; for Prime, Tierce, Sext and None, for each of these, seven; twelve for Vespers, seven for Compline, and let them pray for the dead.

From the feast of All Saints to the Nativity of the Lord, let them fast. As for the holy Lent, from the Epiphany for forty days, which the Lord consecrated by His own holy fast, may those who keep it voluntarily be blessed by the Lord, but let not those, who do not wish to do so, be constrained. Let them fast, however, during the other 

Lent to the Resurrection of the Lord. At other times let them not be bound to fast except on Fridays. In time of manifest necessity, however, let not the brethren be bound to corporal fasting.

PAGE I counsel, warn and exhort my brethren, in the Lord Jesus Christ, that when they are abroad they neither dispute and contend in words, nor judge others, but show themselves gentle, peaceful and modest, well-mannered and humble, speaking courteously to all, as is becoming. 

Unless constrained by evident necessity or weakness they should not ride on horseback. Into whatsoever house they enter, let their first words be: Peace unto this house; and, according to the holy Gospel, they may eat of whatever food is set before them. 

CHAPTER IV. 

[That the Brethren May Not Receive Money] 

I firmly command all the brethren not, in any way at all, through themselves or through an interposed person, to receive coin or money. 

However, for the necessities of the sick, and for the clothing of the other brethren, let the Ministers and Custodes alone, by means of spiritual friends, be carefully solicitous, according to places and times and cold regions, in such wise as they shall deem to be expedient, provided always, as has been said, that they receive neither coin nor money. 

CHAPTER V. 

[On the Manner of Working] 

Let those brethren to whom the Lord has given the grace of working, work faithfully and devoutly, yet in such wise that, excluding idleness which is hurtful to the soul, they do not extinguish the spirit of holy prayer and devotion to which all created things are meant to contribute. In return for their labor they may receive what is necessary for the bodily wants of themselves and their brethren, except coin or money; and this humbly as befits the servants of God and the followers of most holy poverty.

CHAPTER VI. 

[That the Brethren Should Appropriate 

Nothing for Themselves]

Let the brethren appropriate nothing for themselves, neither house nor land nor anything, and, as pilgrims and strangers in this world, serving the Lord in poverty and humility, let them go confidently for alms; and they should not be ashamed of this because the Lord made 

Himself poor in this world for us. This is that summit of highest poverty which constitutes you, my dearest brothers, heirs and princes of the kingdom of Heaven; which has made you poor in earthly things, but

PAGE 

raised you up in virtue. May this be your portion, leading into the land of the living (Psalm 141, 6). Dearest brethren, clinging wholly to this, may you never wish to have aught else here below in memory of Our Lord Jesus Christ. 

Wheresoever the brethren are and meet together, let them show that they are members of the same family, and frankly expose their needs one to another, for if a mother loves and cherishes the son that is born to her, how much more should each one love and cherish his spiritual brother; and if anyone should fall ill, the other brethren ought to serve him as they would wish to be served themselves. 

CHAPTER VII. 

[On Penance to Be Imposed on the Brethren Who Sin] 

If any of the brethren, at the instigation of the enemy, should fall into any of those mortal sins concerning which it has been decided among the brethren that recourse be had to the Ministers Provincial, the said brethren must have recourse to these as soon as they can without delay. Let the Ministers themselves, if they are priests, with mercy impose a penance upon them; but if they are not priests, let them cause a penance to be imposed by others, priests of the Order, as may seem to them most expedient according to God. And they must be careful not to be angry and perturbed on account of anyone's sin, for anger and vexation hinder charity in themselves and in others. 

CHAPTER VIII. 

[Of the Election of the Minister General of This Order, and of the Pentecost Chapter] 

Let all the brethren be bound to have always one of the brethren of this Order as Minister General and servant of the whole Fraternity, and let them be strictly bound to obey him. On his death let the election of a successor be made by the Provincial Ministers and Custodes at the Pentecost Chapter. Let the Provincial Ministers be always bound to assemble for this at whatever place may have been determind by the Minister General. It is to be held once every three years, or at other intervals greater or less, according to what shall have been arranged by the aforesaid Minister. If, at any time, it should become clear to all the Provincial Ministers and Custodes, that the aforesaid Minister is not apt for the service and common welfare of the brethren, let the brethren named above, to whom the election is committed, be bound to choose for themselves another as Custos in the name of the Lord. After the

PAGE 

Pentecost Chapter, the Ministers and Custodes may each, if they wish and deem it expedient, convoke the brethren in their Custodies to a 

Chapter once in the same year.

CHAPTER IX [Of Preachers] 

Let not the brethren preach in the diocese of any bishop, if they have been refused by him permission to do so. None of the brethren should presume to preach at all to the people unless he has been examined and approved by the Minister General of this Order, and has received from him leave to preach. I warn and exhort these same brothers that when they preach their words be well chosen and chaste (Psalm 11, 7), of such sort as to be useful and edifying for the people, proclaiming vice and virtue, punishment and glory, with brevity of speech, for a short word did the Lord make upon the earth (Rom. 9, 28).

CHAPTER X. 

[Of the Admonition and Correction of the Brethren] 

The brethren who are Ministers and servants of the other brethren, should visit their brethren and give them counsel and humbly and charitably correct them, not enjoining anything upon them which may be against their conscience and our Rule. The brethren who are subjects should remember that for the love of God they have renounced their own wills, and so I firmly command them to obey their Ministers in all things which they have promised the Lord to observe, saving only what may be opposed to their conscience or our Rule. 

And wheresoever there are brethren who know and acknowledge that they cannot observe the Rule spiritually, they ought to and may have recourse to their Ministers. Let the Ministers receive them lovingly and kindly, and with such familiar affability of manner as to lead them to speak and treat with them as would masters with their servants; for so indeed it ought to be, that the Ministers be as the servants of all the brethren.

In the Lord Jesus Christ, I warn and exhort the brethren to beware of all pride, vain-glory, envy, of avarice and solicitous interest in the things of this world, of detraction and of murmuring. 

Let not the unlettered strain after learning, but let them consider how they ought to desire above all things to have the spirit of the Lord and His holy operation, to pray always to Him with a pure heart,

PAGE 

to have humility and patience in persecution and in sickness, and to love those who persecute us, censure and speak against us, for the Lord says: Love your enemies, and pray for those who persecute and calumniate you. Blessed are they who suffer persecution for justice sake, for theirs is the Kingdom of Heaven: and he that shall persevere unto the end, he shall be saved.

CHAPTER XI. 

[That the Brethren May Not Enter the Convents of Nuns] 

I strictly command all the brethren that they have no suspicious intimacy or dealings with women, and that they do not enter the convents of nuns, except those to whom special permission has been granted by the Apostolic See. Neither should they become godfathers to men or women lest, on this account, scandal should arise among or about the brethren.

CHAPTER XII. 

[Of Those Who Go Among the Saracens Or Other Infidels] 

Whosoever among the brethren, following divine inspiration, may wish to go among the Saracens and other infidels should ask permission to do so from their Provincial Ministers. To none, however, should the 

Ministers give leave to go, except only to those whom they may deem fit to be sent. Finally under obedience do I lay upon the Ministers the injunction of asking from the Lord Pope for one of the Cardinals of the holy Roman Church to be governor, protector and corrector of this Order, that always subject and submissive at the feet of the same holy Church and steadfast in the Catholic faith, we may observe poverty and humility and the holy Gospel of Our Lord Jesus Christ, which we have firmly promised to do. 

Let it be lawful, therefore, to no one at all to belittle this page of our confirmation, or, with rash audacity, to oppose it. Should anyone, however, dare to attempt this, let him know that he will incure the anger of Almighty God and of His Blessed Apostles Peter and Paul. 

Given at the Lateran, on the twenty-ninth day of November, in the eighth year of our Pontificate.

The First Order 

The First Franciscan Order comprises the three families referred to as the Order of Friars Minor, Friars Minor Conventual, and Friars Minor Capuchin. All three Franciscan families profess the same rule, the text of which appears herewith. Each has its own general Curia in 

Rome, its Minister General, and distinct constitutions interpreting the basic rule.

The Second Franciscan Order POOR CLARES 

The Second Franciscan Order, or Poor Clares, is a religious order of cloistered nuns, founded by St. Francis of Assisi and St. Clare in 1212, and observing the "Rule and Life of the Poor Sisters" of St. Clare. 

Each convent or monastery is autonomous, and under the obedience of a Mother Abbess.

In the United States, the Poor Clares have a total of sixteen monasteries and a membership of almost 400 cloistered nuns and externe sisters. In most of their monasteries there is daily adoration of the 

Blessed Sacrament.

FRANCISCAN NUNS OF THE BLESSED SACRAMENT Adoration Chapel, 4108 Euclid Ave. 

Cleveland, Ohio

Though not popularly called Poor Clares, this community of Franciscan nuns professes the rule of the Second Order of St. Francis; has solemn vows and the papal enclosure; and consists of cloistered nuns and externe sisters, who daily recite the breviary according to the 

Capuchin ordo. 

Adoration Monastery was founded in 1921 at the request of the 

Most Reverend Joseph Schrembs by Franciscan nuns who came from

Vienna. In 1933 a group of nuns from Adoration Monastery in Cleveland founded a similar monastery in Dacca, Bengal, India, having a like purpose, namely perpetual adoration of the Blessed Sacrament.

PART TWO

Rule of the Third Order Regular 

As Embodied in the Constitution "Rerum Conditio"

Pope Pius XI, October 4, 1927 CHAPTER I [Sum and Substance of the Religious Life] The form of life of the Brothers and Sisters of the Third Order 

Regular of St. Francis is this: To observe the holy Gospel of our Lord 

Jesus Christ by living in obedience, chastity, and poverty. 

The Brothers and Sisters, following the example of their Seraphic

Father, promise obedience and reverence to the Lord Pope of the Roman Church. They are bound also to obey their canonically established Superiors in all things that pertain to the general and particular aim of their respective Institute. (From the Rule of the First and Second Order, ch. I.) CHAPTER II [Novitiate and Profession] 

The Brothers and Sisters to be received in the Third Order must be faithful Catholics, not suspect of heresy, firm in obedience to the Roman Church, unmarried, free of debt, sound of body, ready of mind, of clean reputation, and at peace with their neighbors. On all these matters they shall be carefully examined before reception by him who has the faculty to receive them (Rule of the III. Order of Leo X, ch. I.), with due observance of the sacred Canons and the Constitutions of their Institute.

The year of the novitiate must, under the discipline of a Master, have as its purpose to train the mind of the novice in the study of the Rule and the Constitutions, in pious meditation and the love of prayer, in mastering all that pertains to the vows and the virtues, in suitable exercises calculated to root out the germs of vice, to curb the passions and to acquire virtues. Lay novices should moreover be carefully instructed in the Christian doctrine, a special conference being given to them at least once a week. (Canon 565, 1 and 2).

At the end of the time of probation, those who are found fit, should be admitted to profession. PAGE 3

PAGE 

 CHAPTER III [Love of God and Neighbor] 

The obstacles to sanctity being removed by the three holy vows, the Brothers and Sisters should strive to fulfill the Divine Law, which dependeth wholly on the love of God and neighbor. Charity is the soul of all virtues and the bond of perfection. Nothing is better, nothing more effective than charity to mortify the vices, to advance in grace and to attain to the height of all virtues. 

A great sign and aid of the love of Christ is the frequent and even daily approach of the Holy Eucharist, which is at once a sacred banquet and a memorial of His passion. It should also be the endeavor of religious souls frequently to visit and devoutly to venerate our Lord Jesus abiding with us in this admirable mystery; for this is the greatest 

Sacrament of the Church and an inexhaustible fountain of all blessings. 

But the test of the love of God is the practice of charity toward our neighbor. Wherefore charity toward others should appear above all in the true follower of Christ. All his conversation should be guarded, useful and proper; that charity may abound in deed, it must first abound in the heart.

CHAPTER IV [Divine Office, Prayer and Fasting] 

The Brothers and Sisters should perform the Divine Office worthily, attentively and devoutly, according to their Constitutions. Lay members, however, shall say twelve Our Fathers for Matins and Lauds, and five for each of the remaining canonical hours.

They must attend Mass daily unless they are legitimately prevented; and they must see to it that they have a pious priest approved by the Ordinary to preach the word of God to them on certain days and instruct them in penance and virtue (cit. Rule of the III. Order, ch. IV).

They should also daily examine themselves before God on what they have done, said and thought, that is examine their conscience, humbly seeking pardon for their faults, and offering and commending to God their purpose of amendment (cit. Rule, ch. IV).

All should try by frequent meditation on the Passion of Christ to nourish and increase day by day the fervor of their devotion; to follow and imitate their Seraphic Father, so that also they can say with St. Paul: "With Christ I am nailed to the cross." "I live, now not I, but Christ liveth in me" (Gal. 2, 19-20).

PAGE Besides the fasts and abstinences to which all the faithful are obliged, they should observe those which are prescribed in their respective Constitutions, notably on the vigils of the Immaculate Conception and of their Seraphic Father Francis.

CHAPTER V Interior and Exterior Conduct] 

Since the Brothers and Sisters of this Fraternity are called the Order of Penance, they should daily carry the cross of mortification, as becomes true penitents.

It behooves them likewise to refrain from all that is choice in apparel as well as in everything else. And according to the salutary advice of St. Peter, the Prince of the Apostles, they should, after laying aside the other vain ornaments of this world, wear no other bodily ornament but their religious garb (cit. Rule, ch. VI). They are, furthermore, bound to observe the enclosure according to the sacred Canons and their respective Constitutions.

They must also be sparing of words and conversations, which cannot be indulged in freely without sin. Let the conduct of the Brothers and Sisters be such that they may edify all by word and example, remembering that Our Lord has said: "So let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works and glorify your Father who is in Heaven" (Mt. 5, 16). Let them approach everybody with the humble and pious greeting of peace, and bear peace with them not only on their lips but also in their heart (cit. Rule, ch. VI).

CHAPTER VI [Care of the Sick] 

If a Brother or Sister falls ill, no one should refuse to offer his services; but it shall be the duty of the Superiors to provide for the proper care of the patient. The others who are not assigned to this duty, should not hesitate to visit the patient and comfort him with consoling words. Not only to the sick, however, but also to the aged and the otherwise needy should all gladly tender the offices of charity, as becomes the children of the Seraphic Father.

The Superiors especially are bound to admonish the sick Brother or Sister to accept the penance of illness and to be truly reconciled with God, reminding the patient also of the nearness of death, and of the severity of Divine Judgment, as well as of the Divine Mercy (cit. 

Rule, ch. VI).

PAGE 

When a Brother or Sister has departed this life, the Superiors shall see to it that the obsequies are held with great piety (cit. Rule, ch. IX). 

The prescribed suffrages should be faithfully performed for the soul of every deceased member.

CHAPTER VII [Nature and Manner of Work] 

Those who, inspired by the grace of the Holy Ghos, have dedicated themselves to the service of God, should avoid idleness, and give their efforts faithfully and devoutly to the divine praises or the various works of piety and charity (cf. Rule of the II. Order, ch. VII).

The religious should, therefore, comply with their duties for the love of God, and perform what their Superiors require of them, to the best of their ability, devoutly and faithfully, as has been said. Nor should they refuse to perform the humbler tasks that may be imposed on them; on the contrary, following the footsteps of their Seraphic

Father, they should perform them more willingly than other tasks. 

Let all things be done in charity, and let the holy love of God so animate the sentiments of the religious in doing their work that they may labor only for His honor and glory, and fulfill the admonition of 

St. Paul the Apostle: "Whether you eat or drink, or whatsoever else you do, do all to the glory of God" (I Cor. 10, 31).

CHAPTER VIII 

[Obligation of the Rule]

Each and all the articles contained in the present Rule are counsels to help the wayfarers save their souls the more easily; none of them oblige under sin, either mortal or venial, except where one were otherwise obliged to them by human or Divine law (cit. Rule, ch. X). 

The Brothers and Sisters are, however, obliged to perform the penances imposed on them by their Superiors when required to do so. They are also obliged to keep the three essential vows: Poverty, within the limits of their respective Constitution; Chastity, binding themselves to observe celibacy and to refrain under an additional title, namely, that of the vow itself, from every act whatsoever, be it external or internal, that is opposed to chastity; and Obedience, assuming the obligation to obey the command of their lawful Superior according to the tenor of their respective Constitutions (cit. Rule, ch. X).

PAGE All, both Brothers and Sisters, should guide and govern their life according to the law of the religious state which they have professed, and above all faithfully observe what pertains to the perfect fulfillment of their vows. They should have the highest regard especially for those points which direct them to follow the charity and poverty of their Seraphic Father, for it becomes the child most of all to reproduce in itself the image and virtues of its parent. 

CONCLUSION [Blessing of Our Holy Father St. Francis] 

And whosoever will observe these things, may they be filled in 

Heaven with the blessing of the Most High Heavenly Father, and may they be filled on earth with the blessing of His beloved Son, together with the Most Holy Spirit, the Paraclete, and all the Virtues of the 

Heavens and all the Saints. And I, Brother Francis, your little one and servant, as far as I am able, confirm unto you within and without this most holy blessing, which may you enjoy with all the Virtues of the Heavens and all the Saints now and forevermore. Amen. (From the 

Testament of the Seraphic Father.)

The Third Order Regular There is the regular Third Order and the Third Order Secular of 

St. Francis. The regular Third Order comprises communities of men and women living under vows in the convent. The secular Third Order comprises men and women living under a pledge to Mother Church in the world.

The regular Third Order developed out of the secular Third Order, by force of the latter's inherent tendency away from worldliness and toward the things of God. 

Even St. Elizabeth of Hungary, who died in 1231, only ten years after the founding of the Third Order Secular and five years after St.

Francis, is said to have lived a kind of common life with her maids; and by 1241 there are definite records of formal convent communities living according to an adaptation of St. Francis' Tertiary rule (see 

Franciscan Herald and Forum, 1941, pages 133 and 168). 

Communities following the Third Order rule multiplied in the next two centuries. In 1521, Leo X solemnly adapted the Tertiary rule of Nicholas IV of 1289 to convent life and the three vows. In turn 

Pius XI adapted the rule of Leo X to modern conditions by the constitution "Rerum Conditio," of October 4, 1927. He abolished the rule of

Leo X, and made the new adaptation obligatory as the basic rule for all Franciscan Third Order institutes living in the convent under vows. 

To these communities belongs eminently what is known as the 

Third Order Regular of Penance (abbreviated T.O.R.), a clerical papal order with solemn vows, whose headquarters are at Sts. Cosma and 

Damiano, Via in Miranda 2, Rome. It is this community which holds from Benedict XIII (Exponi Nobis, Sept. 30, 1729) equal rights with the First Order in all matters concerning the Third Order Secular. No other (clerical) Franciscan community can enjoy this jurisdiction except by a special concession on the part of the Holy See, a concession which is not per se included in the privilege of aggregation. 

But besides the Third Order Regular specifically so called (T.O.R.), there has always and especially in the past century been so luxuriant a growth of Tertiary convent communities, that Franciscan Tertiarism may well be styled The Fruitful Ideal. In rare instances these communities have solemn vows, the contemplative life, and papal enclosure. 

But almost to a unit these communities have only the simple vows, PAGE 8

PAGE temporal or perpetual, and they are devoted to the active life of public piety and charity. At the same time they evince the characteristic 

Franciscan tendency toward the contemplative life, above all in perpetual adoration.

These many Franciscan Tertiary communities are independent of one another as well as of the jurisdiction of the clerical communities. 

They are one only in pursuing the identical basic rule of Pius XI and the identical ideal of Franciscan piety and charity. That they display a lively interest in all things pertaining to the whole order of St. Francis, and notably in promoting the Third Order Secular, is a natural reaction to their common origin from St. Francis.

Nevertheless, to enjoy to the fullest measure possible the spiritual advantages of the Franciscan order, these several communities secure for themselves what is known as affiliation or aggregation with some established and privileged group of the great Franciscan family, notably with the First Order or with the Third Order Regular specifically so called. Without such aggregation any Franciscan Tertiary community enjoys only such spiritual privileges as may have been granted to it specifically, which usually amounts to no more than is granted to the religious and the faithful in general. But with such aggregation the members of the aggregated community are entitled to all the personal and local indulgences together with the special communion of good works of the aggregating group; likewise to the indults which that group may have obtained for its aggregated regular Tertiaries. 

Such aggregation is generously granted on application to one of the four Father Generals: of the O.F.M., of the O.F.M.Cap., of the

O.M.C., or of the T.O.R. Wherever it could be ascertained, the aggregation of the communities here listed, has been indicated including the date.

The data herewith assigned for the several communities are as of January, 1940, substantially the picture of today. The co-operation of all communities concerned is respectfully but urgently solicited at all times to keep these data up-to-date, complete, and correct. Correspondence for the purpose may be sent to the National Secretariat of the Third 

Order of St. Francis, 3200 Meramec Street, St. Louis, Mo., or to the national organ of all Franciscan Communities, Franciscan Herald and 

Forum, 5045 South Laflin Street, Chicago.

In the present enumeration the clerical communities of men (Priests and Brothers) are listed first; thereafter, the congregations of Brothers.

PAGE 

The communities of regular Tertiary Sisters are grouped in two divisions: Group A, from 1 to 39, are communities founded in the United 

States, or at least having no present jurisdictional ties abroad. Group B, from 40 to 60, are communities operating in the United States, but either founded abroad or having a General Motherhouse outside the United States. In either group precedence is given to the communities having several provinces in the United States.

For convenient reference, a number is assignd to each community. 

The numbers, therefore, indicate no preference whatever. It will be seen that they follow the alphabetical alignment of the main or mother house according to the location of the latter. 

The arrangement and enumeration here presented is copyrighted by the National Secretariat of the Third Order of St. Francis in the 

United States.

Graymoor, New York

The Franciscan Friars of the Atonement are a clerical congregation of regular Tertiaries having both priests and Brothers. Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.). Headquarters of the Very Rev.

The Society works in the interests of the missions here and abroad, and of Church Unity. The so-called Church Unity Octave originated with the Society of the Atonement. In Canada the friars have missions for the Japanese and Ukrainians. Membership, 105.

INSTITUTE OF THE THIRD ORDER OF THE SISTERS OF ST. FRANCIS, PHILADELPHIA FOUNDATION (1) Glen Riddle, Pa. 

General motherhouse and novitiate: Convent of Our Lady of Angels, LaVerna Heights, Glen Riddle, Pa. Aggregated to the First Franciscan 

Order (O.F.M.). The community has four provinces in the United 

States and an Irish procure with headquarters at Mt. Alvernia, Mallow, in the Diocese of Cloyne. The four provinces have an aggregate membership of 1,500 Sisters in 116 establishments.

The community owes its founding in 1855 to the Venerable John 

Nepomucene Neumann, C.SS.R., then Bishop of Philadelphia.

The Sisterhood conducts parochial, commercial and high schools, academies, orphanages, hospitals, a home for boys, houses for the aged, a day nursery, a working girls' home and the domestic department in several institutions of learning. Their establishments include missions among the Colored and the Indians. The Sisters also maintain their own house of studies at the Catholic University, Washington, D. C.

SISTERS OF ST. ELIZABETH 

(19B) Milwaukee, Wisc.

The community is a diocesan institute founded in 1931, and aggre gated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.Cap.). The general mother house and novitiate is located at 101 E. Reservoir Ave., Milwaukee, Wisc., to which is attached St. Elizabeth's Home. The Sisters engage in the care of infirm and convalescent women regardless of age. Membership, 15 at the motherhouse.

General motherhouse and novitiate: St. Joseph's Convent, Monterey, Calif. Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.).

The community has seventeen Sisters, and has no other establishment besides the motherhouse.

Algernan Ave., Prince's Bay, S. I., New York. Total membership, 30 in three establishments.

This is the only community of Franciscan Sisters all of whom are Colored. They conduct a grammar school, a day nursery, and do settlement work in the Harlem district of New York.

(44) Amarillo, Tex.-Pasto, Colombia

THE FRANCISCAN SISTERS OF BALTIMORE CITY 

(46) Baltimore, Md.-Mill Hill, London

The general motherhouse of the congregation is in its English Province-St. Mary's Abbey, Mill Hill, London, N. W. 7, England. 

Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.). 

A second province, known as the African Province, has three receiving houses: Holme Hall, Holme on Spalding Moor, York, England;

Franciscan Convent, Dundalk, Ireland, and Franciscan Convent, Nsambya, Brit. East Africa-headquarters of the African Missions in Uganda.

The American Province has its motherhouse and novitiate at: Convent of Our Lady and St. Francis, 2226 Maryland Ave., Baltimore, Md.

The work of the community in the United States is exclusively among the Colored-educational work and homes caring for youth. Total membership in the U. S., 105 in six establishments. The Sisters have establishments in four dioceses.

(49) Chestnut Hill, Newton, Mass.-Rome, Italy

FRANCISCAN SISTERS OF CALAIS 

(53) Monroe, La.-Calais, France

Through its general motherhouse and novitiate in Calais, France, the community is aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.Cap.). 

The community conducts charitable institutions in various countries outside the homeland: in Belgium, Holland, Portugal, Asia, Africa, Argentine (S. A.), Ireland, Madagascar.

The American foundation numbers 25 Sisters in two establishments: 

St. Francis Sanitarium, Monroe, La.-to which is attached a training school for nurses. A branch of this institution is O. L. of the Lake 

Sanitarium, P.O. Box 1109, Baton Rouge, La.

FRANCISCAN SISTERS OF ST. ELIZABETH 

(53B) Newark, N. J.

A congregation with a general motherhouse in Italy, founded by 

Fr. Louis a Cassoria (Suore Elisabettina Franciscana). The mother house of the American foundation is at 41 Gobel St., Newark, N. J. 

Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.).

The Sisters are active as parish visitors and conduct day nurseries and settlement houses. Membership, 20 in two other establishments besides the motherhouse: 

Day Nursery and Community Center, 201 Emmet St., Newark, N. J., and the Chapel at O. L. of Mt. Carmel, 475 East 115 St., New 

York City.

General motherhouse and novitiate in Breslau, Silesia. Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.). The American foundation has its superior at St. Mary's College, Northeast, Pa. Membership, 20.

FRANCISCAN MISSIONARIES OF MARY (56) Providence, R. I.-Rome 

A world-wide congregation popularly called the White Nuns. Aggregated to the First Franciscan Order (O.F.M.) through the general motherhouse in Rome, 12 via Giusti. Its 7,200 members and 310 houses are divided into a number of provinces according to countries of Europe, Asia, Africa, Japan, Oceania, South America, Canada. The American.

Province of St. Francis of Assisi has its motherhouse and novitiate at 

399 Fruit Hill Ave., North Providence, R. I.

PAGE The community observes perpetual adoration in a number of its houses, and conducts elementary schools and charitable homes of various descriptions. Membership in the United States, 230 in 12 establishments. 

The Sisters are represented in eight dioceses.

OF ST. FRANCIS  A REPORT ON THE NATIONAL ORGANIZATION SUBMITTED TO ITS FIFTH NATIONAL CONGRESS By FR. MAXIMUS POPPY, O. F. M. 

SECRETARY, NATIONAL EXECUTIVE BOARD, T. O. s. F. 

OFFICE OF THE NATIONAL SECRETARY 3200 MERAMEC STREET, ST. LOUIS, MO. 

1941

Introduction Arrangement and Terminology 

The national directory of the Third Order, first published in 1931, is herewith presented in its third edition. By and large it follows the plan adopted for the 1935 edition: that of listing Fraternities by dioceses as well as by states and cities. The canonical status of the Third Order brings it into more or less frequent official relations with the diocesan authorities.

Canonical headquarters of the Fraternity is indicated by quoting the church or institution at which it is established. To cite the Fraternity's official name, especially where the patron's name differs from that of its canonical headquarters, would prove confusing. Accordingly, when the Fraternity has to communicate with provincial or national headquarters, it is recommended that for purposes of identification it employ, besides a signature, the legend "The Third Order at the Church of ,” adding city and state.

Business headquarters of the Fraternity is synonymous with the mailing address as quoted in these pages. Throughout the mailing address of the Spiritual Director is given. Since this address is not easily subject to change, it is deemed the safest channel of communication with the Fraternity and its officers. For the same good reason no names of persons appear. Instead, the office of the provincial headquarters is quoted. 

Spiritual direction and affiliation is more clearly indicated in the present edition than in previous ones. The Visitor of a given province is the official of the First Franciscan Order or of the Third Order Regular named in the rule, and reference to the Fraternity's particular jurisdiction in each case should make for greater clarity.

Membership figures for the individual Fraternity have been omitted in favor of an aggregate membership of the province. In a work published at infrequent intervals, totals published for each province sufficiently reflect the local scene, and answer their purpose for business between the headquarters of the provinces and the National Organization.

The membership figures cited are those submitted in the annual report to the Executive Board by the Third Order Commissaries, and, as of September 30, 1940. In most cases, the reports distinguish between active and inactive members.

The term group has been introduced into the present edition to denote any major division in the membership entailing either separate meetings, or officers or spiritual directors—regardless of whether they are canonically established in their own right or not.

A further improvement in the present edition is the appendix, with its two listings. The first is a register of the Fraternities found in the body of the directory, grouped according to provinces. The other lists isolated Fraternities. This measure was decided upon by the National 

Executive Board and the Third Order Commissaries at their annual meetings in 1939 and 1940.

The compiler has exercised due diligence in securing official and first-hand information. However, corrections and supplemental information will be gratefully accepted.

The Rule of the Third Order of St. Francis SECULAR 

As Embodied in the Constitution "Misericors Dei Filius" 

Leo XIII, May 30, 1883

The reference to the papal constitution of Leo XIII is made by way of indicating that the Tertiary rule of life is a part of the Church's legislation.

This standard English translation of the rule was approved at the Second National Congress of the Third Order, held October 3 to 6, 

1926, in New York City.

In virtue of their authority to interpret the spirit of the Third Order rule for the Tertiaries (Canon 702; 1), the Provincial Superiors at the 

Third National Congress, held in 1931, in San Francisco, enjoined instructions on several points of the rule which admit a stricter or more lenient interpretation: on amusements in general, on dancing in particular; on the wearing of the scapular and dispensation in point; on transfers from one Fraternity to another; on the guarantee of the annual visitation as a condition for establishing a new Fraternity. (“Survey of a Decade,” p. 587.) 

At the Fourth National Congress, held in 1936, in Louisville, Ky., the Three-Point Program of Franciscan poverty was ratified: 

1. To commit no sin in heart or hand for the sake of goods of fortune. 

2. To observe moderation in acquiring and enjoying goods of fortune. 

3. To administer all goods of fortune in a way that they will serve the common good no less than personal advantage. "Social Ideals of St. Francis." (Herder 1938).

CHAPTER I Reception, Novitiate, and Profession 

1. Only those may be received as members who have completed their fourteenth year, and are of good character, peace-loving, and above all of tried fidelity in the practice of the Catholic Faith and in loyalty to the Roman Church and the Apostolic See. 

2. Married women may not be received without the husband's knowledge and consent unless their confessor judges otherwise. PAGE 5

PAGE 

3. The members shall wear the small scapular and cord as prescribed; if they do not, they deprive themselves of the rights and privileges of the Order. 

4. All who enter the Order must pass the first year in probation; then they shall duly make their profession upon the Rule of the Order, pledging themselves to observe the Commandments of God and of the Church, and to render satisfaction if they have failed against their profession.

CHAPTER II Rule of Life 

1. In all things let the members of the Third Order avoid extremes of cost and style, observing the golden mean suited to each one's station in life. 

2. Let them with the utmost caution keep away from dances and shows which savor of license, as well as from all forms of dissipation. 

3. Let them be temperate in eating and drinking, and devoutly say grace before and after meals. 

4. They shall fast on the Vigil of the Immaculate Conception and on that of St. Francis; they are to be highly commended who, according to the orginal Rule of the Tertiaries, also either fast on Fridays or abstain from flesh-meat on Wednesdays. 

5. They shall approach the Sacraments of Penance and of the 

Holy Eucharist every month. 

6. Tertiaries among the clergy, since they recite the Divine Office daily, shall be under no further obligation in this regard. Lay members who recite neither the Canonical Hours, nor the Little Office of the 

Blessed Virgin Mary, shall say daily twelve Our Fathers, Hail Marys, and Glorys, unless they are prevented by ill health. 

7. Let those who are entitled to make a last will and testament, do so in good time. 

8. In their daily life let them strive to lead others by good example and to promote practices of piety and good works. Let them not allow books or publications which are a menace to virtue, to be brought into their homes, or to be read by those under their care.

PAGE 

9. Let them earnestly maintain the spirit of charity among themselves and towards others. Let them strive to heal discord wherever they can. 

10. Let them never take an oath except when necessary. Let them never use indecent language or vulgar jokes. Let them examine their conscience every night whether they have offended in this regard; if they have, let them repent and correct their fault. 

11. Let those who can do so, attend Mass every day. Let them attend the monthly meetings called by the Prefect. 

12. Let them contribute according to their means to a common fund, from which the poorer members may be aided, especially in time of sickness, or provision may be made for the dignity of Divine Worship. 

13. Let the officers either personally visit a sick member, or send someone to perform the services of charity. In case of serious illness, let them remind and urge the sick person to arrange in time the affairs of his soul. 

14. At the funeral of a deceased member the resident and visiting 

Tertiaries shall assemble and say in common five decades of the Rosary for the soul of the departed. Moreover, let the priests at the Holy 

Sacrifice and the lay members, if possible, having received Holy Communion, pray with fervent charity for the eternal rest of the deceased. 

CHAPTER III 

Offices, Visitation, and the Rule Itself 1. The offices shall be conferred at a meeting of the members. 

The term of these offices shall be three years. Let no one without good reason refuse an office tendered him, and let no one discharge his office negligently. 

2. The Visitor, who is charged with the supervision of the Order, shall diligently investigate whether the Rule is properly observed. Therefore it shall be his duty to visit the Fraternities every year, or oftener if need be, and hold a meeting, to which all the officers and members shall be summoned. Should the Visitor recall a member to his duty by admonition or command, or impose a salutary penance, let such member meekly accept the correction and not refuse to perform the penance. 

3. The Visitors are to be chosen from the First Franciscan Order or from the Third Order Regular, and shall be appointed by the

PAGE  

provincial or local superiors when requested. Laymen cannot hold the office of Visitor. 

4. Disobedient and harmful members shall be admonished of their duty a second and a third time; if they do not submit, let them be dismissed from the Order. 

5. Those who offend against any provision of this Rule, do not incur the guilt of sin unless in so doing they also transgress the Commandments of God or of the Church. 

6. Should a just and serious cause prevent a member from observing any provision of the Rule, such person may be dispensed therefrom, or the regulation may be prudently commuted. For this purpose the ordinary superiors of the First and Third Order Regular, as also the aforesaid Visitors, shall have full power.

The National Third Order Organization 

Since the First National Congress of Tertiaries held in 1921, the Fraternities have united in Tertiary Provinces, and these in turn have federated into a national Tertiary organization known officially as 

The Third Order of St. Francis in the United States.

Its constitution and by-laws were formally ratified at the Second National Congress, held in New York City in 1926. The text appearing in these pages is amended as of 1941, and supersedes any other published at a previous date.

In 1941, the Administrative Board of Bishops of the National Catholic Welfare Conference designated the Most Reverend Christian H. 

Winkelmann, S.T.D., Bishop of Wichita, as Bishop Protector of the 

Third Order of St. Francis in the United States.

The office of the National Secretary (3200 Meramec St., St. Louis, Mo.) is designed to serve as a clearing house for information respecting the Third Order in all its phases, to dispatch its inter-provincial business, and to maintain working arrangements with allied religious movements. 

Organs of communication for the National Third Order Organiza tions are: The annual meeting of its Executive Board and Third Order 

Commissaries after the close of its fiscal year (September 30); a national congress every fifth year; the magazine Franciscan Herald and Forum (founded in 1922—published monthly at 5045 S. Laflin St., Chicagosubscription, $1.00).

NATIONAL CONSTITUTION OF THE THIRD ORDER OF ST. FRANCIS  

[Name] 

ARTICLE I 

This National Tertiary Organization shall be known as the Third Order of St. Francis in the United States. For brevity's sake, it is in this Constitution also called the "National Organization."

PAGE 9

PAGE 

 ARTICLE II 

[Objects] The objects of this National Organization are to further the full observance of the Rule of the Third Order, i. e., to promote a truly 

Christian life according to the spirit of St. Francis, notably: 

1. To foster national union and co-operation among the Tertiary 

Provinces, the Fraternities, Directors and members of the Third Order. 

2. To co-operate in augmenting religious, charitable and social work of the Fraternities. 

ARTICLE III 

[Government] The National Organization shall be presided over by two Boards of officers, called the National Directive Board and the National Executive Board.

ARTICLE IV [The National Directive Board] 

All Provincials and Commissaries of the three branches of the 

First Order and of the Third Order Regular constitute the National Directive Board. 

No new measure of concerted action of the National Organization shall be adopted without the previous approval of the National Directive Board.

ARTICLE V [The National Executive Board] The members of the National Executive Board shall be as follows: 

1. Three members of the First Order, namely, one Friar Minor, one Friar Minor Conventual, one Friar Minor Capuchin, and one member of the Third Order Regular—of whom one shall receive appointment as Chairman, one as Vice-Chairman, and one as Secretary, by the 

National Directive Board. 

2. Three Consultors, namely one secular Tertiary priest and two 

Tertiary laymen, who shall be elected at each National Convention. 

At the first session of the Board thus elected one of the number of Consultors will be elected National Treasurer.

PAGE ARTICLE VI [Duties of the Executive Board and its Officers] 

It shall be the duty of the National Executive Board to promote the good and welfare of the National Third Order Organization, to guide the National Convention, and to co-operate in carrying out the program of action that will be adopted at such National Convention. 

The Executive Board shall also attend to all business that may arise when the National Convention is not in session. 

The Chairman shall call the meetings of the National Executive 

Board and preside thereat.

The Vice-Chairman shall preside in the absence of the Chairman. 

The Secretary shall— 

1. Keep minutes of the meetings of the Board; 

2. Keep records and statistics of the Tertiary Provinces and Fraternities belonging to the National Organization; 

3. Urge non-affiliated Fraternities by correspondence to affiliate with a Tertiary Province of their choice and to enter the National Organization; 

4. Send reports of the meetings of the National Executive Board to all Provincials, Commissaries and Directors of the Third Order as also to the press in as far as it may be to good purpose. 

5. At the expiration of his term of office transfer to his successor all records, books, documents and official correspondence. 

The Treasurer shall receive all monies belonging to the National Organization, giving a receipt therefor. He shall keep an account of all monies received and disbursed. 

He shall annually or whenever called upon by the Executive Board submit a detailed financial statement. 

He shall give such security as the Directive Board may require and at the expiration of his term of office deliver to his successor all monies and property belonging to the National Organization. 

The Consultors will co-operate with the other officers in carrying out the purpose of the National Organization.

PAGE 

 ARTICLE VII [Meetings of the Executive Board] 

A meeting of the National Executive Board must be held at least once a year. In case of inability of one of the Board to attend the meeting, the respective Provincial shall appoint a substitute. 

Each Province of the First Order or the Third Order Regular shall be represented at the annual National Board meeting by the Commissary of the respective Tertiary Province, or his duly appointed substitute, with a deciding vote.

ARTICLE VIII 

[Vacancies] A vacancy in any office caused by death, resignation or otherwise among the members of the Executive Board of the First and Third Order Regular shall be filled by the respective Provincial; a vacancy among the consultors shall be filled by the Executive Board at their next meeting.

ARTICLE IX 

[Membership] The National Organization consists primarily of the Tertiary Provinces of the United States.

A Fraternity is in good standing if it is canonically erected, regularly has its meetings and its canonical visitation.

ARTICLE X [National Meetings] 

(a) Every five years a National Convention of the Third Order of St. Francis in the United States shall be held at the time and place designated by the National Directive Board and communicated to the National Executive Board within twelve months after the last Convention. The Convention shall be under the auspices of a different 

Province each time. (b) If the National Directive Board finds it advisable to change the time and place of the Convention, such change shall be announced at least one year in advance of the time previously designated.

PAGE (c) A National Convention shall not be called in a city without the previously obtained consent of the Ordinary of the city and the 

Provincials concerned. (d) After consultation with the respective Provincials, the National 

Executive Board selects the Reverend Chairman, Vice-Chairman, and at least three members of the local Convention Board, and a Convention. 

Secretary, who shall be chosen from the Province which has charge of the Convention. (e) The National Executive Board shall arrange the general plan, select subjects and speakers, supervise the meetings and determine the number of votes to which each Fraternity represented in the Convention is entitled.

Every Fraternity may have as many representatives in the Convention as it chooses, who shall have a voice in the deliberations. But each 

Fraternity shall be entitled to only one voting delegate for every one hundred members or fraction of this number. Furthermore, each member of the hierarchy and each prelate present, each member of the National Directive Board and of the National Executive Board, each Fraternity 

Director present, shall be entitled to one vote. (f) The Local Convention Board shall attend to all arrangements according to instructions given by the National Executive Board. 

ARTICLE XI [Sectional Meetings] 

Annual conventions in various centers and sectional meetings of members of the Third Order at frequent intervals are strongly recommended and the National Executive Board shall co-operate with the various Provincials to foster and systematize such meetings. 

ARTICLE XII 

[Autonomy] In furthering the objects of the Third Order of St. Francis in the 

United States, the National Organization shall not in any way interfere with the government, or disturb the autonomy of any Third Order 

Fraternity, or group of Fraternities.

In the case of Fraternities aggregated to a Tertiary Province, the National Executive Board shall send reports of its meetings directly

PAGE 

to the Directors, but shall receive the statistics and collect the financial dues through the Tertiary Province headquarters. 

ARTICLE XIII [National Finances] 

In order to carry on its work, the National Organization shall receive an annual contribution of five cents per capita from each Tertiary 

Province. 

After every National Third Order Convention, the monies not expended shall go to the National Third Order Treasury.

Extraordinary expenses shall not be made without the consent of the National Directive Board. 

On travel to and from meetings of the National Executive Board mileage only will be refunded to its members from the National Third 

Order Treasury. 

ARTICLE XIV [Emergencies] 

In case of any financial or other emergency, the National Executive Board shall apply for instructions to the National Directive Board. 

ARTICLE XV [Change of Constitution] 

Any proposed amendment to this Constitution must first be submitted in writing to the National Executive Board at least six months before a National Convention.

After due deliberation, the National Executive Board shall report on the proposed amendment to the National Directive Board at least three months before said Convention.

If the National Directive Board approve thereof, the amendment shall be submitted to the National Convention for final decision.

BY-LAWS ARTICLE I 

The National Tertiary Organization is placed under the special protectorate of the Sacred Heart of Jesus and of Mary Immaculate.

PAGE Every year within the month of the Sacred Heart, the Reverend Third Order Directors will have their Tertiaries publicly renew their consecration to the Sacred Heart.

ARTICLE II 

The order of the meetings of the National Executive Board shall be as follows: 

1. Call to order and prayer; 

2. Roll call; 

3. Reading of the minutes; 

4. Remarks by the Chairman; 

5. Report of the Secretary; 

6. Financial report; 

7. Reports of Committees; 

8. Reports of instructions from the National Directive Board; 

9. The good and welfare of the National Tertiary Organization; 

10. Recommendations of Commissaries and Directors; 

11. Unfinished business: 

12. New business; 

13. Appointments of Committees to report at the next meeting; 

14. Closing prayer and adjournment.

ARTICLE III 

A bond of at least $5,000.00 shall be furnished by the Treasurer of the National Executive Board and the premium shall be paid out of the 

National Treasury. 

ARTICLE IV 

Any amendment, change or revision of the By-Laws shall be subject to the requirements of Article XV of this Constitution.

PAGE  ARTICLE V 

For the purpose of dispatching business between the National Directive and Executive Boards, and of safeguarding the prerogatives of the 

Directive Board, the office of Secretary of the National Directive Board is established, to-wit: One of the Provincial Superiors is designated as liaison officer in communicating to his fellow-Provincials the business of the Third Order Secular requiring their approval and sanction.

ISOLATED FRATERNITIES 

These Third Order centers are listed to complete the record. They are termed isolated as in the case of individual Tertiaries who are out of touch with the Third Order body-contrary to the Tertiary Rule.

In the case of some Fraternities affiliation with the Tertiary province could not be ascertained since the 1936 national congress; in others communication with headquarters of their jurisdiction has been suspended, or they do not appear on the official roster of Fraternities reported to the Central Office by their respective Tertiary province. "The Visitor, who is charged with the supervision of the Order, shall diligently investigate whether the Rule is properly observed. Therefore it shall be his duty to visit the Fraternities every year, or oftener if need be, and hold a meeting, to which all the officers and members shall be summoned. "The Visitors are to be chosen from the First Franciscan Order or from the Third Order Regular, and shall be appointed by the provincial or local superiors when requested."-The Rule, Chapter III, par. 2, 3.

 TERTIARY PROVINCE (1) OF THE ASSUMPTION 

Under the jurisdiction of the Franciscans (O.F.M.) of the Province bearing the same name. Its affiliated Fraternities are by and large Polishspeaking groups. The Third Order Commissary's office issues a monthly homiletic service to its directors and maintains a Third Order department in the monthly magazine "Miesiecznik Franciszkanski." 

Total: 288 groups; combined membership 10,000.

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, Franciscan Friary, 

Pulaski, Wisc. CALIFORNIA

 TERTIARY PROVINCE (2) OF THE IMMACULATE CONCEPTION 

Under the jurisdiction of the Franciscans (O.F.M.) of the Province bearing the same name. Its Fraternities are about equally divided between 

English and Italian-speaking groups. Since 1931, the Tertiary Province has been operating under its own Constitution; it has a board of provincial officers; two conventions of the province have been held at intervals of three years. 

Total: 61 groups; combined membership 10,000.

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, 255 Carroll St.,

TERTIARY PROVINCE (3) OF ST. JOHN THE BAPTIST 

Under the jurisdiction of the Franciscans (O.F.M.) of the Province bearing the same name. The province adopted a constitution in 1924, and for the further guidance of its Fraternities it has published "Director's Manual" and "Fraternity Book." The board of provincial officers meets annually. The Tertiary Province has held two conventions and periodically regional meetings of Fraternity directors. 

Total: 54 groups; combined membership 5,000. 

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, 1615 Vine St., Cincinnati, Ohio.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (5) OF THE SACRED HEART 

Under the jurisdiction of the Franciscans (O.F.M.) of the Province bearing the same name. The Tertiary Province, founded in 1917, operates under a constitution and has a code of regulations for Fraternities known as "Manual of Regulations." Under the terms of its constitution the province has held nine conventions at three-year intervals, its provincial board holding meetings annually. 

Total: 134 groups; combined membership 12,800.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (6) OF SANTA BARBARA 

Under the jurisdiction of the Franciscans (O.F.M.) of the Province bearing the same name. The province was founded in 1924; it has a constitution and a manual of regulations for its Fraternity officers; a bi-monthly medium of communication with its affiliates, "Western Tertiary." Under the terms of its constitution the province held six conventions at two and three-year intervals; annual meetings of its provincial board, and periodically regional meetings of Tertiaries and directors. 

Total: 51 groups; combined membership 8,000.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (7)-COMMISSARIATE OF THE HOLY LAND 

This unit of the Franciscan Order (O.F.M.) has but one foundation, the Monastery known as Mt. St. Sepulchre, Brookland, Washington,

D. C. The community does not engage in the usual parochial activities, and is therefore limited to the two groups of Tertiaries established at 

Mt. St. Sepulchre. 

Total: 2 groups; combined membership 500.

PAGE 

Present headquarters: Mt. St. Sepulchre, Brookland, Washington,

D. C.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (10) OF ST. JOSEPH Under the jurisdiction of the Capuchin Franciscan (O.F.M.Cap.) 

Province of the same name. The Tertiary Province was established in 

1922. The "Normae" serves as a provincial constitution, and the “Officers' 

Guide" regulates the management of local Fraternities. The province is incorporated under a state charter for administering several properties and institutions of a charitable and educational character, and holds annual meetings of its board of directors in New York. Four provincial conventions have been held at intervals of three and four years. 

Total: 52 groups; combined membership 13,700.

MINORITE PROVINCE (15) OF THE IMMACULATE CONCEPTION Under the jurisdiction of the Friars Minor Conventual (O.M.C.) 

of the Province bearing the same name. The Tertiary Province, extending at the time to the units now in the Province of O. L. of Consolation (16), was organized in 1925. Since 1925 "Tertiary Director's Guide" served both as a provincial constitution and Fraternity officers' manual, until in 1940 the present constitution was adopted. The province holds a plenary convention the second year after a national congress, and regional conventions of its "custodies" the year preceding a national congress. The office of the Commissary communicates with its constituents through the medium of "The Tertiary Adviser," quarterly, or when business justifies its issuance. This province has since 1936 specialized in the establishment and regular visitation of Lithuanian Fraternities in the Eastern States. A number of its Fraternities are among Slovak-speaking Tertiaries. 

Total: 51 groups; combined membership 3,500.

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, Tekakwitha Friary, 

R. 1, Fonda, N. Y.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (16) OF O. L. OF CONSOLATION 

Under the jurisdiction of the Friars Minor Conventual (O.M.C.) of the Province bearing the same name. In 1926, the then western portion of the Province of the Immaculate Conception became a Province in its own right with headquarters in Louisville, Ky. Its Tertiary Province has a constitution and a guide for Fraternity officers embodied in the "Manual of Regulations." Also, it has its special "Manual of the Archconfraternity of the Cord of St. Francis." For regular communication with the Fraternities the Commissary conducts a Third Order department in the monthly magazines "The Companion" and "Apostolate of Our Lady." Periodically the directors of its several regions meet in conference. 

Total: 26 groups; combined membership 1,200.

PAGE 

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, 118 South Ninth 

St., Terre Haute, Ind.

TERTIARY PROVINCE (23) OF ST. ANTHONY OF PADUA 

Under the jurisdiction of the Friars Minor Conventual (O.M.C.) of the Province bearing the same name. The Tertiary Province dates since 1939, when the corresponding province of the First Order was established. The affairs of the Tertiary Province and its Fraternities are provisionally regulated by the "Manual of Regulations" of Province (5). The Fraternities are exclusively among Polish-speaking Tertiaries. 

Total: 31 groups; combined membership 1,500. 

Present headquarters: Third Order Commissary, Mt. St. Lawrence, Becket, Mass.
